\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

By combining Eqs. (
159
), (
161
), and Table
5
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Lambda_{i,j},\Lambda_{i,j}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{d^{4}}{s^{4}}d^{2}B\wp+\frac{d^{3}}{s^{3}}d^{2}B\wp+\frac{d}{s}d^{2}B\wp+\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}d^{2}B\wp+d^{2}B\wp+\frac{(d+s)^{4}}{s^{4}}dB+\frac{d(d+s)}{s^{2}}dB\sqrt{d\wp}+\frac{d^{2}(d+s)}{s^{3}}B\sqrt{d\wp}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{(d+s)}{s}dB\sqrt{d\wp}+\frac{(d+s)^{3}}{s^{3}}B\sqrt{d\wp}+\frac{d(d+s)^{2}}{s^{3}}dB+\frac{(d+s)^{2}}{s^{2}}dB\sqrt{d\wp}+\frac{d^{2}(d+s)^{2}}{s^{4}}dB\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{4}}{s^{4}}+\frac{d^{3}}{s^{3}}+\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)d^{2}B\wp}}\right],

(164)

which confirms Lemma
15
.
∎

Appendix G
Proof of Theorem
1

To prove Theorem
1
, it suffices to prove the following theorem,
which is a stronger version of Theorem
1
.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose
s\geq 1
is the number of systems collectively measured in our learning phase;
accuracy parameters
0<\delta,\epsilon<1
;

\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{M}
is a collection of quantum states chosen from
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
,
with
\max_{l}\Tr(\rho_{l}^{2})=\mathscr{P}
;
and
O_{1},\dots,O_{M}
is a collection of observables chosen from
\mathrm{Obs}(B)
, with
1\leq B\leq d
.
To estimate all
M
properties
\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O_{l}\,\mathcal{U}(\rho_{l})}}\right)

within additive error
\epsilon
and failure probability at most
\delta
,
the number of queries required by our protocol for CSEU is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\mathscr{P}+s\min\{1,B\mathscr{P}\}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{\max\{d^{2},s^{2}\}\sqrt{B\mathscr{P}}}{s\epsilon}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M}{\delta}}}\right)}}\right).

(165)

Proof of Theorem
4
.

First, we consider the case of
M=1
, namely, the goal is to accurately predict

\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)
for an observable
O\in\mathrm{Obs}(B)
and a quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
that has purity
\wp=\Tr(\rho^{2})
. Suppose we want the probability in Eq. (
26
) to be at most
1/4
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{Z}_{(r)}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|\geq\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{4}.

(166)

Thanks to Proposition
1
, it is sufficient to have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\wp}{s}+\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,B\wp}}\right\}}}\right)\leq\epsilon^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{4}}{s^{4}}+1}}\right)B\wp\leq\epsilon^{2},

(167)

where we ignored constant coefficients. These two conditions can be achieved by choosing

\displaystyle q=C\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d\wp}{s},\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,B\wp}}\right\}}}\right\}+\frac{\sqrt{B\wp}}{\epsilon}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}},1}}\right\}\bigg)

(168)

for some constant
C
.

Recall that our final estimate is the median value
\hat{E}(O,\rho)
given in Eq. (
15
).
Assume that the number of batches
R
is an odd number, then
\hat{E}(O,\rho)
is actually some
\hat{Z}_{(r)}(O,\rho)
.
If
\hat{E}(O,\rho)
is a bad estimate, i.e.,

\big|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)\big|\geq\epsilon
,
then at least half of the
R
estimates

\hat{Z}_{(r)}(O,\rho)
are bad.
Hence, when Eq. (
166
) holds for all
r=1,2,\dots,R
,
the failure probability of
\hat{E}(O,\rho)
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|\geq\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\#\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{r:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{Z}_{(r)}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|\geq\epsilon}}\right\}\geq\frac{R}{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j=0}^{(R-1)/2}\binom{R}{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3}{4}}}\right)^{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{4}}}\right)^{R-j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-R\,D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{(R-1)/2}{R}\,}}\right\|\,3/4}}\right)}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}\mathrm{e}^{-R\,D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/2\|3/4}}\right)},

(169)

where
(a)
holds because the
R
estimates

\hat{Z}_{(r)}(O,\rho)
are independent;
D(x\|y):=x\ln\frac{x}{y}+(1-x)\ln\frac{1-x}{1-y}
is the Kullback-Leibler divergence;
(b)
follows from the Chernoff bound in Lemma
16
below; and
(c)
holds because
D(x\|y)
is strictly decreasing in
x
when
0<x<y<1
.
Therefore, to ensure that the failure probability in Eq. (
169
) is at most
\delta
,
it suffices to choose

\displaystyle R=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\ln\delta^{-1}}{D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/2\|3/4}}\right)}}}\right\rceil=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\delta^{-1}}}\right).

(170)

By Eqs. (
168
) and (
170
),
the total number of queries used by our protocol is

\displaystyle s\cdot q\cdot R=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\wp+s\min\{1,B\wp\}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{\max\{d^{2},s^{2}\}\sqrt{B\wp}}{s\epsilon}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{-1}}}\right)}}\right),

(171)

which confirms Theorem
4
in the special case
M=1
.

To complete the proof, now we consider the general case of
M\geq 1
.
According to the union bound, if our protocol can accurately predict one expectation value
\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)
with failure probability at most
\tilde{\delta}
, then it can also accurately predict
M
expectation values

\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O_{l}\,\mathcal{U}(\rho_{l})}}\right)
for
l=1,2,\dots,M
simultaneously,
with failure probability at most
M\tilde{\delta}
.
This fact and the query number in Eq. (
171
) together confirm Theorem
4
in the general case
M\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 16

(Chernoff bound)
.

Suppose
0<p<1
, integers
k,z\geq 0
, and
k\leq pz
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{z}{j}p^{j}(1-p)^{z-j}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-zD\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\!\frac{k}{z}}}\right\|p}}\right)}.

(172)

Appendix H
Proof of Lemma
4

Note that
\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)\geq\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\delta
is satisfied if the following relations hold:

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{E}}}[\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)]\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},\ \operatorname{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)-{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)}}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}\right\}\leq\delta.

(173)

Here and afterwards, the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}
is over the randomness in our protocol.

First, we have

\displaystyle\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)

\displaystyle:=\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|E(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}.

(174)

It follows that

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{{\mathbb{E}}}\sqrt{\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]}.

(175)

Second, Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)-{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)}}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\epsilon^{2}}{\Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)}}\right]\leq\frac{4}{\epsilon^{2}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\epsilon^{2}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}}\right],

(176)

where the last inequality follows from Eq. (
174
) again.

According to equations (
173
), (
175
), and (
176
),
the relation
\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Xi(\mathcal{T},O)\geq\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\delta
is satisfied if

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]\leq\frac{\delta\epsilon^{2}}{4}.

(177)

In addition, we have

\displaystyle\,{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\Big[{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)}}\right|^{2}-\hat{E}(O,\rho)^{*}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\hat{E}(O,\rho)\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)+[\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)]^{2}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{E}(O,\rho)}}\right|^{2}}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{E}(O,\rho)}}\right]}}\right|^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\rho\sim\mathcal{T}}{\rm{Ave}}\;{\Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{E}(O,\rho)}}\right],

(178)

where
(a)
holds because
{{\mathbb{E}}}[\hat{E}(O,\rho)]=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\,\mathcal{U}(\rho)}}\right)
.
Equations (
177
) and (
178
) together confirm Lemma
4
.

Appendix I
Additional results on Hamiltonian learning

I.1
Performance of previous protocols for learning an arbitrary Hamiltonian

To illustrate the advantage of our Hamiltonian learning protocol, here we provide more details
on the performance of previous protocols for learning an
n
-qubit unknown Hamiltonian
H
, including those in Refs.
[
24
,
66
,
71
,
70
,
74
,
36
,
37
]
.
These protocols are highly efficient when
H
has a sparse or local structure.
However, we show that none of them can learn an arbitrarily complex
H
with respect to the normalized Frobenius norm (NFN) using only

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{3})
Hamiltonian queries or total evolution time, even with access to quantum memory.
Actually, most previous works did not consider the problem of learning fully arbitrary Hamiltonians, and
only a few existing protocols can be modified to learn a completely unknown Hamiltonian under our framework.

The Hamiltonian
H
of interest can be expressed as

\displaystyle H=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}}\mu(\mathbf{q})\sigma_{\mathbf{q}},

(179)

where
\sigma_{\mathbf{q}}
are Pauli operators and
\mu(\mathbf{q})\in\mathbb{R}
are Pauli coefficients.

H
is called a
k
-body Hamiltonian with
M
terms

if
\mu(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
only for
M
Pauli terms
\sigma_{\mathbf{q}}
with
{\rm wt}(\sigma_{\mathbf{q}})\leq k
,
where the weight
{\rm wt}(\sigma_{\mathbf{q}})
denotes the number of qubits on which
\sigma_{\mathbf{q}}
acts nontrivially.
Most previous Hamiltonian learning protocols focus on estimating all non-zero Pauli coefficients of
H
.
That is, they aim to output accurate estimates
\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})
for
\mu(\mathbf{q})
that satisfy

\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
with high success probability. Here

\bm{\mu}:=(\mu(\mathbf{q}))_{\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}}
and
\hat{\bm{\mu}}:=(\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q}))_{\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}}

are the vectors of true coefficients and estimated coefficients, respectively, and

\displaystyle\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{p}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}}|\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})-\mu(\mathbf{q})|^{p}}}\right)^{1/p}

(180)

denotes the
\ell^{p}
norm of
\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}
.
Let
\hat{H}=\sum_{\mathbf{q}}\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})\sigma_{\mathbf{q}}
be the learned description of
H
,
then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\|\hat{H}-H\|_{2}=\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{2}\leq d\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{\infty},

(181)

where the inequality is saturated when
|\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})-\mu(\mathbf{q})|
are equal for all
\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}
.
Therefore, estimating all Pauli coefficients of
H
within additive error
\epsilon

only implies learning
H
within error
d\epsilon
in NFN.

Reference
[
24
]
proposed an efficient protocol for learning the Pauli coefficients of an arbitrary traceless Hamiltonian
H
.
It uses parallel queries to the time evolution under
H
in experiments, and thus needs quantum memory.
To achieve
\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
with high probability, this protocol requires
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n\|H\|^{4}\epsilon^{-4})
queries to
H
and a total evolution time of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n\|H\|^{3}\epsilon^{-4})
.
A similar performance is achieved by the protocol in Ref.
[
71
]
, which uses sequential queries to
H
.
According to the above discussions, if the goal is to learn
H
within error
\epsilon
in NFN, then
the query number and the evolution time required by these protocols are both
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{4}\epsilon^{-4})
, assuming that
\|H\|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(n))
.

A recent work Ref.
[
66
]
proposed a protocol
for learning a
k
-body Hamiltonian
H
with
M
terms, which is robust to state preparation and measurement (SPAM) errors.
It employs sequential queries to
H
in each experiment and achieves the following performance:
By using
N=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(3^{k}M)
independent experiments and a total evolution
time
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(9^{k}M^{1/p+1/2}\epsilon^{-1})
, it can output estimate
\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})
for every
\mu(\mathbf{q})
such that
\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{p}\leq\epsilon
with high probability, where
p=1
or 2.
This protocol is highly efficient when
k
is a constant.
However, if we do not make prior assumptions about the structure of
H
, then
k
and
M
can take values up to
k=n
and
M=d^{2}
.
For this worst-case scenario, learning
H
within error
\epsilon
in NFN
(that is,
\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon
) requires a

T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(9^{n}d^{2}\epsilon^{-1})\approx\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{5.170}\epsilon^{-1})
total evolution time
and
N=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(3^{n}d^{2})\approx\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{3.585})
experiments.
Another work
[
70
]
showed that one can achieve

\|\hat{\bm{\mu}}-\bm{\mu}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
from

T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(M^{2}\epsilon^{-1})
total evolution time.
From the above discussions, this result implies a
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{5}\epsilon^{-1})
time complexity
for learning an arbitrary Hamiltonian within error
\epsilon
in NFN.

Reference
[
74
]
proposed an efficient protocol for learning an unknown
Hamiltonian
H
with
M
Pauli terms.
In order to learn
H
within error
\epsilon
in NFN, it needs

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(M\|H\|^{2}\epsilon^{-2})
Hamiltonian queries, which reduces to
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{2}\epsilon^{-2})

if
H
is an arbitrary unknown Hamiltonian satisfying
\|H\|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(n))
.
However, this protocol requires not only the time evolution unitary
\operatorname{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Ht}
, but also the backwards evolution unitary
\operatorname{e}^{\mathrm{i}Ht}
, which is physically unrealizable.

There are also Hamiltonian learning protocols that do not use quantum memory and look beyond Hamiltonians with sparse interactions
[
36
,
37
]
.
To learn all coefficients of a Hamiltonian
H
within additive error
\epsilon
,
the protocol of Ref.
[
37
]
requires
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(M^{4}\epsilon^{-2})
queries,
and thus is unsatisfactory when
H
contains exponentially many terms;
the protocol of Ref.
[
36
]
requires
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(9^{k}\epsilon^{-2})
queries,
and thus is unsatisfactory when
H
contains high-weight terms.
From the previous discussions, when the goal is to learn an arbitrary Hamiltonian within error
\epsilon
in NFN, the query costs of these protocols are more than
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{5}\epsilon^{-2})
.

Compared with these existing protocols, our Hamiltonian learning protocol demonstrates superior performance
by simultaneously (i) operating without the requirement of quantum memory, and
(ii) achieving a smaller query number and total evolution time (see Theorem
3
in the main text),
making it favorable for real-world implementation.

I.2
Details of our Hamiltonian learning protocol and its performance

Given time evolution access to an arbitrary
n
-qubit traceless Hamiltonian
H
with
\|H\|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(n))
.
Our goal is to learn a classical description
\hat{H}\in\mathcal{L}_{\rm H}(\mathcal{H})
of the Hamiltonian,
such that the following condition holds:

\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{H}-H}}\right\|_{2}\leq\epsilon}}\right\}\geq 1-\delta

(182)

for accuracy
0<\delta,\epsilon<1/3
.
Next, we present our learning protocol for this task in Appendix
I.2.1
and
show its performance in Appendix
I.2.2
.

I.2.1
Hamiltonian learning protocol

We follow the Hamiltonian learning protocol sketched in Sec.
VII.2
of the main text
with the following parameter choices:
Our evolution time in each experiment is set to be
t_{j}=\tfrac{T}{2}(1+z_{j})
for
j=1,2,\dots,L
, where

\displaystyle T=\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|H}}\right\|},\qquad z_{j}=-\cos\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2j-1}{2L}\pi}}\right),

\displaystyle L=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil 2\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{8\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|H}}\right\|}{\sqrt{2\pi}\ln(2)\varepsilon}}}\right)}}\right\rceil,\qquad\varepsilon=\frac{\epsilon}{d}.

(183)

In the first step of our protocol, for each time
t_{j}
(
1\leq j\leq L
), we treat
\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}
​ as an unknown unitary channel, and implement our CSEU protocol in Sec.
III
to estimate the expectation values
\langle\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\rho_{\mathbf{q}}(t_{j})}=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}\,\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}\!(\rho_{\mathbf{q}})}}\right]
,
where the input state
\rho_{\mathbf{q}}
and the measured observable
\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}
are chosen as follows
(see also Ref.
[
24
, Lemma 6.1]
):
Let
1\leq i\leq n
be such that
\mathbf{q}_{i}\neq 0
; let
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}\backslash\{\mathbf{q}_{i}\}
and set
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}_{j}=0
for all
j\neq i
; let

\rho_{\mathbf{q}}:=\frac{1}{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}[\sigma_{\mathbf{q}},\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}]}}\right).

(184)

We denote by
2\hat{\alpha}_{t_{j}}^{(\mathbf{q})}\in\mathbb{R}
our estimate of
\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}\,\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}(\rho_{\mathbf{q}})}}\right]
, which should achieve the following accuracy:

\displaystyle\Pr\Big\{\,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{2\hat{\alpha}_{t_{j}}^{(\mathbf{q})}-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}\,\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}(\rho_{\mathbf{q}})}}\right]}}}\right|\leq\tilde{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\forall\,\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}\backslash\{0^{n}\}\Big\}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{L},

(185)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\varepsilon}:=\frac{3T\varepsilon}{4(L-1)L(2L-1)}.

(186)

Let
1\leq s\leq d
be the number of systems collectively measured by our CSEU protocol in each experiment.
As explained in Sec.
III
, if
s>1
, then quantum memory is required.
If instead
s=1
, then the CSEU protocol does not need quantum memory and ancillary qubits;
in each round of experiments,
it requires only the preparation of a computational-basis state
|0\rangle^{\otimes n}
, the application of a random Clifford unitary to a single system, a measurement in the computational basis,
and one application of
\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}
​ [ i.e., one real
\mathcal{O}(1/\|H\|)
-time evolution under
H
].

In the second step of our Hamiltonian learning protocol, we perform Chebyshev polynomial interpolation
[
37
,
24
]
to estimate the Pauli coefficients
\mu(\mathbf{q})
by using
\hat{\alpha}_{t_{j}}^{(\mathbf{q})}
learned in the first step.
The Chebyshev coefficients are estimated as

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{0}^{(\mathbf{q})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=1}^{L}\hat{\alpha}_{t_{j}}^{(\mathbf{q})},

(187)

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{l}^{(\mathbf{q})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{L}\sum_{j=1}^{L}\hat{\alpha}_{t_{j}}^{(\mathbf{q})}\,T_{l}(z_{j}),\quad 1\leq l\leq L-1,

where
T_{l}
is the
l
th Chebyshev polynomial.
They in turn give rise to the first-order Taylor coefficients
[
37
,
24
]

\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q}):=-\frac{2}{T}\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}(-1)^{l}\,\hat{b}_{l}^{(\mathbf{q})}\,l^{2},\quad\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}\backslash\{0^{n}\}.

(188)

Here
\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})
serves as our estimate of
\mu(\mathbf{q})
.
Finally, we use these estimated values to construct our learned description:

\hat{H}=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}\backslash\{0^{n}\}}\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})\sigma_{\mathbf{q}}.

(189)

I.2.2
Performance of the protocol

The performance of our Hamiltonian learning protocol in the previous subsection
is stated in the following theorem,
which is a stronger version of Theorem
3
in the main text.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose the unknown
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
H
to be learned is traceless and satisfies
\|H\|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(n))
;
parameters
0<\delta,\epsilon<1/3
;
and
s
is the number of systems collectively measured in each experiment.
With the accuracy guaranteed in Eq.
(
185
)
,
the learned description
\hat{H}
in Eq.
(
189
)
satisfies Eq.
(
182
)
.
When
s=1
, our Hamiltonian learning protocol is ancilla-free and dose not use quantum memory;
its query number to
H
and total evolution time both scale as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigg(\frac{d^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{d^{3}}{\epsilon}\bigg)\log(\delta^{-1})}}\right).

(190)

When
s=\Theta(\sqrt{d})
, our Hamiltonian learning protocol uses quantum memory; its query number to
H
and total evolution time both scale as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2.5}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log(\delta^{-1})}}\right).

(191)

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We begin by proving that
\hat{H}
given in Eq. (
189
) indeed satisfies Eq. (
182
).
First, by the union bound, the accuracy guaranteed in Eq. (
185
) implies that
the following condition holds with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{2\hat{\alpha}_{t_{j}}^{(\mathbf{q})}-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}\,\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}(\rho_{\mathbf{q}})}}\right]}}}\right|\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad\forall\,\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}\backslash\{0^{n}\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ 1\leq j\leq L.

(192)

Second, according to Ref.
[
24
, Appendix D]
,
if Eq. (
192
) holds with
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{3T\varepsilon}{4(L-1)L(2L-1)}
, then

\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})
constructed in Eq. (
188
) is an
\varepsilon
-accurate estimate of
\mu(\mathbf{q})

for all
\mathbf{q}\neq 0^{n}
, i.e.,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{q})-\mu(\mathbf{q})}}}\right|\leq\varepsilon\quad\forall\,\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{n}\backslash\{0^{n}\}.

(193)

Third, as explained in Sec.
VII.2
, if Eq. (
193
) holds with
\varepsilon=\epsilon/d
, then
\hat{H}
in Eq. (
189
) satisfies

\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{H}-H}}\right\|_{2}\leq\epsilon.

(194)

In conclusion, our protocol learns the unknown Hamiltonian within error
\epsilon
in NFN, with success probability at least
1-\delta
.

Next, we calculate the number of queries to
H
and total evolution time required by our protocol.
Notice that the Hamiltonian
H
is queried only in the first step of our protocol,
in which we use the CSEU method to estimate linear properties of the time-evolution channel
\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}
.
Let
1\leq s\leq d
be the number of systems collectively measured by our CSEU protocol in each experiment.
According to Theorem
4
in Appendix
G
, for each
1\leq j\leq L
,
in order to guarantee the relation (
185
), our CSEU protocol
needs to query
\mathcal{U}_{t_{j}}
​ ( i.e., the Hamiltonian
H
) the following number of times:

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{s}{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{d^{2}}{s\tilde{\varepsilon}}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4^{n}-1}{\delta/L}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{sd^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{d^{3}}{s\epsilon}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{-1}}}\right)}}\right],

(195)

where we have used the relations
\Tr(\rho_{\mathbf{q}}^{2})=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/d}}\right)
and
\Tr(\sigma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}^{2})=d
,
and the assumption
\|H\|=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(n))
.
In the following, we consider two cases depending on the value of
s
.

1.

First, when
s=1
, our protocol does not use quantum memory and ancillary qubits.
In this case, the total number of Hamiltonian queries is given by

\displaystyle N_{\rm total}=LN_{1}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{d^{3}}{\epsilon}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{-1}}}\right)}}\right],

(196)

and the total evolution time under
H
reads

\displaystyle T_{\rm total}

\displaystyle=N_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{L}t_{j}\leq N_{1}LT

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{d^{3}}{\epsilon}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{-1}}}\right)}}\right].

(197)

Equations (
196
) and (
197
) confirm Eq. (
190
) in Theorem
5
.

2.

Second, when
s=\Theta(\sqrt{d})
, our protocol uses quantum memory.
In this case, the total number of queries to
H
is

\displaystyle N_{\rm total}

\displaystyle=LN_{1}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}\,\Theta(\sqrt{d})}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{d^{3}}{\Theta(\sqrt{d})\,\epsilon}}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{-1}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2.5}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log(\delta^{-1})}}\right),

(198)

and the total evolution time reads

\displaystyle T_{\rm total}=N_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{L}t_{j}\leq N_{1}LT=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2.5}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log(\delta^{-1})}}\right).

(199)

Equations (
198
) and (
199
) confirm Eq. (
191
) in Theorem
5
.

∎

Appendix J
Proof of Eq. (
61
)

J.1
Main proof

When
\rho
is the infinite temperature thermal state
I/d
, and
W
,
V
are unitary, traceless, and Hermitian operators,
the estimator
\hat{C}(U)
defined in Eq. (
60
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\hat{C}(U)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{m(m-1)d}\sum_{i\neq j}\hat{D}(i,j),

\displaystyle\hat{D}(i,j)

\displaystyle:=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{X}_{i}\otimes\hat{X}_{j}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)T_{(1,3)}}}\right].

(200)

So the variance of
\hat{C}(U)
can be expanded as

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{C}(U)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{k\neq\ell}\Cov\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j),\hat{D}(k,\ell)}}\right]}{m^{2}(m-1)^{2}d^{2}}.

(201)

We need to bound all of these covariance terms to bound the variance.
When all indices
i,j,k,\ell
are distinct, the covariance is 0 by independence.
For the other cases, the covariance can be bounded by

\displaystyle\Cov\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j),\hat{D}(k,\ell)}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,\ell)^{*}}}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(k,\ell)^{*}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,\ell)}}\right]-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\mathbb{E}}\big[\hat{D}(i,j)\big]}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,\ell)}}\right].

(202)

Here the second equality holds because
\hat{D}(k,\ell)
is real. To see this, note that

\displaystyle\hat{D}(k,\ell)^{*}

\displaystyle=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[T_{(1,3)}^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)^{\dagger}\big(\hat{X}_{k}\otimes\hat{X}_{\ell}\big)^{\dagger}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{X}_{k}\otimes\hat{X}_{\ell}}}\right)T_{(1,3)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{D}(k,\ell),

(203)

where the second equality holds because
\hat{X}_{k}
,
\hat{X}_{\ell}
,
W
,
V
, and
T_{(1,3)}
are all Hermitian operators;
and the last equality holds because
W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}
and
T_{(1,3)}
commute.

The following lemma summarizes the upper bounds for
{\mathbb{E}}\big[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,\ell)\big]
in different cases.

Lemma 17
.

For each combination of
i,j,k,\ell
with
i\neq j
and
k\neq\ell
,
the term
{\mathbb{E}}\big[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,\ell)\big]
is

1.

\mathcal{O}\Big(\big(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1\big)d^{2}\Big)

when exactly one index matches
(|\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}|=1)
.

2.

\mathcal{O}\Big(\big(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1\big)^{2}d^{4}\Big)

when both indices match
(|\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}|=2)
.

For all combinations of
i,j,k,\ell\in\{1,2,\dots,m\}
with
i\neq j
and
k\neq\ell
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{4})
combinations where all four indices
i,j,k,\ell
are distinct;

\mathcal{O}(m^{3})
combinations where exactly one index matches
(|\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}|=1)
;
and
\mathcal{O}(m^{2})
combinations where both indices match
(|\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}|=2)
.
These facts, together with Eqs. (
201
), (
202
) and Lemma
17
, imply that

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{C}(U)}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{m^{2}(m-1)^{2}d^{2}}\,\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[m^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)d^{2}+m^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)^{2}d^{4}}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\frac{1}{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+1}}\right)+\frac{d^{2}}{m^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{4}}{s^{4}}+1}}\right)}}\right],

(204)

which confirms Eq. (
61
) in the main text.

J.2
Proof of the auxiliary Lemma
17

To complete the proof of Eq. (
61
), in the following we prove Lemma
17
,
which bounds the term
{\mathbb{E}}\big[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,\ell)\big]
for all combinations of
(i,j,k,\ell)
with
i\neq j
and
k\neq\ell
.
In particular, Appendix
J.2.1
deals with the case in which exactly
one index matches
(|\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}|=1)
;
Appendix
J.2.2
deals with the case in which both indices match
(|\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}|=2)
.

J.2.1
Proof of the first statement in Lemma
17

Suppose indices
i,j,k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
are all distinct. Then we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(i,k)}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big\{\!\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\hat{X}_{i}\otimes\hat{X}_{j})(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top})T_{(1,3)}}}\right]\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\hat{X}_{i}\otimes\hat{X}_{k})(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top})T_{(1,3)}}}\right]\Big\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\hat{X}\otimes\Upsilon_{\mathcal{U}})(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top})T_{(1,3)}}}\right)}}\right]^{2}\Big\},

(205)

where the second equality holds because
{\mathbb{E}}[\hat{X}_{j}]={\mathbb{E}}[\hat{X}_{k}]=\Upsilon_{\mathcal{U}}
[see Eq. (
10
) in the main text]
and that
\hat{X}_{i}
,
\hat{X}_{j}
,
\hat{X}_{k}
are independent snapshots.
Drawing tensor-network diagrams, we have

\displaystyle\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\hat{X}\otimes\Upsilon_{\mathcal{U}})(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top})T_{(1,3)}}}\right]

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ =\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)}}\right].

(206)

It follows that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(i,k)}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Tr\!\big[\hat{X}(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top})\big]}}\right)^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{X}\otimes\hat{X}}}\right]\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)}}\right).

(207)

The expression of
{\mathbb{E}}\big[\hat{X}\otimes\hat{X}\big]
in Lemma
11
has 7 terms.
Plugging them into Eq. (
207
), we get

\displaystyle\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tilde{\Delta}_{\ell}\,(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top})}}\right]=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{2}}}\right)&\ell=1,2,3,4,\\
0&\ell=5,6,7,\end{cases}

(208)

which can be derived using tensor-network diagrams.
For example,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tilde{\Delta}_{2}\,(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes WUVU^{{\dagger}}W\otimes V^{\top})}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{12}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Tr(V^{2})\Tr[(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W)^{2}]+2\Tr[(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W)^{2}UV^{2}U^{{\dagger}}]}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{12}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\Tr(I))^{2}+2\Tr(I)}}\right]=\frac{d^{2}+2d}{12}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{2}}}\right),

(209)

where
(a)
holds because
\mathrm{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V}}\right)=\Tr(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W)=0
,
and
(b)
holds because
(WUVU^{{\dagger}}W)^{2}=V^{2}=I
.

Equations (
207
), (
208
), and Lemma
11
together imply that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(i,k)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2d^{2}(d+1)^{2}(d+s)}{s^{2}(d+s+1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}+\frac{s}{\kappa_{3}}+\frac{s}{\kappa_{3}}+\frac{s(s-1)}{2\kappa_{4}}}}\right)\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{2}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)d^{2}}}\right].

(210)

In addition, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(k,i)}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(i,k)}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)d^{2}}}\right],

(211)

where the first equality holds because
\hat{D}(k,i)=\hat{D}(i,k)
by symmetry.
Equations (
210
) and (
211
) together confirm the first statement in Lemma
17
.

J.2.2
Proof of the second statement in Lemma
17

Suppose indices
i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
are distinct. Then we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(i,j)}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\hat{X}_{i}\otimes\hat{X}_{j})(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top})T_{(1,3)}}}\right)}}\right]^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{X}\otimes\hat{X}}}\right]\otimes{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{X}\otimes\hat{X}}}\right]}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)T_{(1,5)(3,7)}}}\right].

(212)

Define

\displaystyle\gamma(\mu,\nu):=\Tr\Big[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\Delta}_{\mu}\otimes\tilde{\Delta}_{\nu}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}\otimes W\otimes V^{\top}}}\right)T_{(1,5)(3,7)}\Big],

(213)

which can be bounded via tensor-network diagrams:

\displaystyle\gamma(\mu,\nu)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{4}}}\right)&\mu,\nu\in\{1,2,3,4\},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(214)

The proof of Eq. (
214
) is a simple analog of Eq. (
208
) and is omitted.

According to Lemma
11
, we can then expand the RHS of Eq. (
212
) as

RHS of Eq. (
212
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{d^{4}}{s^{4}}\gamma(1,1)+\frac{d^{3}}{s^{3}}[\gamma(1,2)+\gamma(1,3)+\gamma(2,1)+\gamma(3,1)]+\frac{d}{s}[\gamma(2,4)+\gamma(3,4)+\gamma(4,2)+\gamma(4,3)]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}[\gamma(2,2)+\gamma(2,3)+\gamma(1,4)+\gamma(3,2)+\gamma(3,3)+\gamma(4,1)]+\gamma(4,4)+\frac{(d+s)^{4}}{s^{4}}\gamma(7,7)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{d(d+s)}{s^{2}}[\gamma(3,5)+\gamma(3,6)+\gamma(2,5)+\gamma(2,6)+\gamma(5,2)+\gamma(5,3)+\gamma(6,2)+\gamma(6,3)]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{d^{2}(d+s)}{s^{3}}[\gamma(1,5)+\gamma(1,6)+\gamma(5,1)+\gamma(6,1)]+\frac{(d+s)}{s}[\gamma(4,5)+\gamma(4,6)+\gamma(5,4)+\gamma(6,4)]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{(d+s)^{3}}{s^{3}}[\gamma(5,7)+\gamma(6,7)+\gamma(7,5)+\gamma(7,6)]+\frac{d(d+s)^{2}}{s^{3}}[\gamma(2,7)+\gamma(3,7)+\gamma(7,2)+\gamma(7,3)]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{(d+s)^{2}}{s^{2}}[\gamma(5,5)+\gamma(5,6)+\gamma(6,5)+\gamma(6,6)+\gamma(4,7)+\gamma(7,4)]+\frac{d^{2}(d+s)^{2}}{s^{4}}[\gamma(1,7)+\gamma(7,1)]\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)^{2}d^{4}}}\right],

(215)

where the last equality follows from Eq. (
214
).
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(j,i)}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{D}(i,j)\hat{D}(i,j)}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{d}{s}+1}}\right)^{2}d^{4}}}\right],

(216)

where the first equality holds because
\hat{D}(j,i)=\hat{D}(i,j)
.
This equation confirms the second statement in Lemma
17
.
